If they’ve changed over time, when did they start?\n\nPatient: No, it hasn't been a month yet—it's been exactly three weeks since the crash. All of this started the very next day and hasn't let up at all, and honestly, it feels like it's been much longer because of how exhausting it is.\n\nDoctor: Since the crash, have there been any periods of at least a day or two when these symptoms mostly went away, or have they been present almost continuously the whole three weeks?"

Manifestations\n- S027 (Sleep_Disturbance): Ever since the crash, I either lie there for hours replaying it in my head or bolt awake at three or four in the morning from these half‑dreams of cheering, I was just standing there feeling flat and disconnected, like I was watching it on TV instead of being there, and I couldn’t tap into any real joy about it.\n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): I’ve been snapping at people over nothing—snarling at my wife because she asked if I was okay, and barking at my staff for small mistakes in the safety reports—then feeling stupid about it a few minutes later but not sure how to stop it in the moment.\n- S079 (Hypervigilance): Whether I’m driving on side streets or just walking around that festival, my eyes are constantly flicking to every car, exit, and siren, my shoulders are tight like I’m bracing for another impact, and I can’t seem to switch that off long enough to actually relax.\n▸ Additional the crash—has been going on ever since that freeway pileup about three weeks ago.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Since that freeway crash about three weeks ago, I feel constantly on edge, like I’m scanning for danger all the time and can’t relax, especially when I’m out driving. It's just making it really hard to get through my normal routine.\n\nDoctor: Since the crash, have you been having unwanted memories, nightmares, or flashbacks about it, or mostly just feeling generally worried and tense? Also, are you avoiding reminders like driving or the freeway?\n\nPatient: Yes, during the day I'll suddenly see the injured driver's bloody face and hear the crunch of metal, and at night I keep waking up from half-dreams of screeching brakes. I've also been completely avoiding the freeway, even rearranging my work schedule to take side streets because driving makes me feel so incredibly tense and on guard.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms been going on continuously for more than one month since the crash, or has it only been about three weeks so far? If they’ve changed over time, when did they start?\n\nPatient: No, it hasn't been a month yet—it's been exactly three weeks since the crash. All of this started the very next day and hasn't let up at all, and honestly, it feels like it's been much longer because of how exhausting it is.\n\nDoctor: Since the crash, have there been any periods of at least a day or two when these symptoms mostly went away, or have they been present almost continuously the whole three weeks?\n\nPatient: No, there haven't been any breaks at all. It has been pretty much continuous for the entire three weeks, and I haven't had a single day where things felt back to normal. I've just been constantly on edge without any real relief.\n\nDoctor: Just to clarify, are your symptoms still ongoing right now, and if they continue, will it soon be more than one month since the crash? That timing helps me tell whether this fits an acute stress reaction or post-traumatic stress disorder.\n\nPatient: Yes, they are still happening every single day and haven't gotten any better. Since the crash was three weeks ago, if this keeps up for another week, it will be a full month. Right now, it doesn't feel like it's letting up at all.\n\nDoctor: Before this crash, had you ever experienced a similar traumatic event that caused the same kinds of symptoms for more than a month? Or is all of this only tied to this crash three weeks ago?\n\nPatient: No, I've never gone through anything like this before, and I've never had these kinds of symptoms in the past. This is all completely new to me and only started after that freeway crash three weeks ago.\n\nDoctor: Have these symptoms now lasted longer than one full month since the crash, or is it still under a month? If they have passed one month, are the nightmares, intrusive memories, avoidance, and feeling on guard still happening?\n\nPatient: No, it is still under a month; it has been exactly three weeks since the crash. All of these symptoms, like the bad sleep, the flashes of the crash, and feeling constantly on edge, are still happening very actively right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any moments during the crash or afterward where you felt detached, numb, dazed, or like things weren’t real? That can help me tell whether this is more of an acute stress reaction versus symptoms that may develop into PTSD.\n\nPatient: Yes, I’ve felt really numb and disconnected from everything lately. For example, at my kid's soccer game, everyone was cheering but I just felt completely flat, like I was watching it on TV rather than actually being there. I just couldn't feel any real joy about it.\n\nDoctor: Did that detached or unreal feeling start right during the crash or immediately afterward, and has it been happening repeatedly since then? Or is it more just a general emotional numbness that developed later?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure of the exact moment it started or if I felt that way during the actual crash itself. Lately, though, I just feel this flat, disconnected numbness—like I'm watching my life on TV rather than actually being there—and I can't seem to feel any real happiness or joy.\n\nDoctor: Since the crash, have you had times when things felt unreal or dreamlike, or you felt outside your body watching yourself? Or is it mainly emotional numbness without that unreal/out-of-body feeling?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both. I feel completely numb and unable to feel any real joy, but I also have this strange feeling of being disconnected and watching my own life happen from the outside, almost like I'm watching a TV show instead of actually being there.\n\nDoctor: Right during the crash or in the minutes immediately after, did you feel dazed, like things weren’t real, or as if you were outside your body? Or did that disconnected feeling only start later in the days after the crash?"

I said or even how the fire trucks got there, it’s just a blank between a few sharp moments.\n- S076 (Inability_Positive_Emotions): Even when my kid’s team won their soccer game and everyone was cheering, I was just standing there feeling flat and disconnected, like I was watching it on TV instead of being there, and I couldn’t tap into any real joy about it.\n- S077 (Irritability_Aggression): I’ve been snapping at people over nothing—snarling at my wife because she asked if I was okay, and barking at my staff for small mistakes in the safety reports—then feeling stupid about it a few minutes later but not sure how to stop it in the moment.\n- S079 (Hypervigilance): Whether I’m driving on side streets or just walking around that festival, my eyes are constantly flicking to every car, exit, and siren, my shoulders are tight like I’m bracing for another impact, and I can’t seem to switch that off long enough to actually relax.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: In the last few weeks I’ve had to shuffle my whole work schedule to avoid freeway drives and I catch myself zoning out in safety meetings, and socially I’ve bailed early on things like that community festival because I feel too overwhelmed to stay and actually participate.\n- duration: All of this—being on edge, the bad sleep, the flashes of the crash—has been going on ever since that freeway pileup about three weeks ago.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Since that freeway crash about three weeks ago, I feel constantly on edge, like I’m scanning for danger all the time and can’t relax, especially when I’m out driving. If they’ve changed over time, when did they start?\n\nPatient: No, it hasn't been a month yet—it's been exactly three weeks since the crash. All of this started the very next day and hasn't let up at all, and honestly, it feels like it's been much longer because of how exhausting it is.\n\nDoctor: Since the crash, have there been any periods of at least a day or two when these symptoms mostly went away, or have they been present almost continuously the whole three weeks?\n\nPatient: No, there haven't been any breaks at all. That timing helps me tell whether this fits an acute stress reaction or post-traumatic stress disorder.\n\nPatient: Yes, they are still happening every single day and haven't gotten any better. Since the crash was three weeks ago, if this keeps up for another week, it will be a full month. That can help me tell whether this is more of an acute stress reaction versus symptoms that may develop into PTSD.\n\nPatient: Yes, I’ve felt really numb and disconnected from everything lately. If they have passed a month, are the nightmares, intrusive memories, avoidance, and feeling on edge still happening?\n\nPatient: It is still less than a month; it has been about three weeks since the freeway pileup. Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.
